### 57. A whole case, from the first question to the name

*Unit Four · rev 3 · Draft: not yet read by a newcomer · Part 5 of 5 · Card 57 of 62*

[reviewers only: card kind `worked`, id `worked-invoice`]

You have the nine names and the two questions about money. Before the drill, watch three cases being run from the top, in the order that the questions are asked. You are not asked anything until the end of each.

*The compost supplier*

> Noor runs a small plant nursery and pays her supplier, Greenfield Compost, every month by ACH transfer. An email arrives from the supplier's usual address: 'Please pay this month's invoice of $1,260. Our bank has changed, so the account on the attached invoice is the new one; please do not use the old one.' No deadline is given.

**Question 1 of 3: What is it asking you to do right now?**

What it is for: sorts a message, a call or an offer by the next thing it asks of you: something on your device, a way into an account, money, facts about you, or nothing at all. Each kind of request puts something different at risk, is guarded by a different habit, and leads to a different set of names. The story that comes with it, who it says it is from, and what the sender is after in the end do not change the answer: what it asks you to do does. You can answer it from the message or the call itself, at the moment the request is made. Where a request asks for two of these things, the answer is the one higher in the list, because it reaches further: a program or a view of your screen reaches everything on the device, a way into an account reaches what the account holds, and money is gone once it is sent.

> Noor runs a small plant nursery and pays her supplier, Greenfield Compost, every month by ACH transfer. An email arrives from the supplier's usual address: '⟦Please pay this month's invoice of $1,260⟧. Our bank has changed, so the account on the attached invoice is the new one; please do not use the old one.' No deadline is given.

Answer: **“Pay or send money”**

The email asks Noor to pay: “Please pay this month's invoice of $1,260”. It asks for no program, no file, no sign-in and no facts about her, so nothing earlier in the list applies. The answer is **“Pay or send money”**.

Still possible: all nine names this unit teaches.

**Question 2 of 3: What does the request say the money is for?**

What it is for: sorts requests for money by the reason they give, which leaves one name or a few. Each scam that asks for money comes with its own kind of reason, and a real request comes with an ordinary one: a bill, a fine or a tax, or a deal you are in. So the reason leaves one name, or a few for the next question to tell apart. You can answer it from the request itself, at the moment it is made.

> Noor runs a small plant nursery and ⟦pays her supplier, Greenfield Compost, every month by ACH transfer⟧. An email arrives from the supplier's usual address: 'Please pay this month's invoice of $1,260. Our bank has changed, so the account on the attached invoice is the new one; please do not use the old one.' No deadline is given.

Answer: **“A bill from someone you already pay”**

The request is about something that she already pays for: “pays her supplier, Greenfield Compost, every month by ACH transfer”. That is a bill from someone she already pays, so the answer is **“A bill from someone you already pay”**. This is the answer that three names share, which is why a second question is needed.

Still possible: **Real payment request**, **Invoice fraud** and **Fake payment link**. Ruled out: **Romance scam**, **Pig-butchering scam**, **Advance-fee scam**, **Recovery scam**, **Fake official scam** and **Overpayment scam**.

**Question 3 of 3: What does it ask you to do with the money?**

What it is for: tells apart the names that share a reason for paying, by what the request asks you to do with the money. Each scam asks for the money in its own way, and a real request asks for it in an ordinary way that holds up when you check it. You can answer it from the request itself, before any money leaves your account.

> Noor runs a small plant nursery and pays her supplier, Greenfield Compost, every month by ACH transfer. An email arrives from the supplier's usual address: 'Please pay this month's invoice of $1,260. ⟦Our bank has changed, so the account on the attached invoice is the new one⟧; please do not use the old one.' No deadline is given.

Answer: **“Pay into new bank account details sent by message”**

Now ask what the email asks her to do with the money: “Our bank has changed, so the account on the attached invoice is the new one”. The account is not the one that she has always paid. That is **“Pay into new bank account details sent by message”**, and one name is left: **Invoice fraud**. Nothing in it is hurried or threatening, and that is why it is easy to miss.

Still possible: **Invoice fraud**. Ruled out: **Romance scam**, **Pig-butchering scam**, **Advance-fee scam**, **Recovery scam**, **Real payment request**, **Fake payment link**, **Fake official scam** and **Overpayment scam**.

**Name it:** **Invoice fraud**

**You are asked:** The invoice is for the right work and the right amount, from the supplier’s usual address, so it can look like a real request. Why is this **Invoice fraud** and not **Real payment request**? Every statement below is true of the case. Before you read the reason, choose the one that settles it.

- (a) The invoice is for the amount that Noor expects, and it comes from the usual address.
- (b) It tells her to pay into an account that is not the one she has paid into every month, and it arrived by message.
- (c) No deadline is given.

**Shown as soon as you choose.** The one that settles it is (b): It tells her to pay into an account that is not the one she has paid into every month, and it arrived by message.
- If you chose (a): True, and it is why the case can look like **Real payment request**. But a copy can send the right amount from a thread that it has gotten into, so these do not settle which of the two this is.
- If you chose (c): True, and a real request gives you time. But a copy that wants to look routine does the same, so it does not settle it.

**Why this is Invoice fraud and not Real payment request**

For **Real payment request** you must be able to point to this: a bill, a fine or a deal that you can tie to something you already had or started, the amount you agreed or owe, details you have always paid or were given at the start, and a request that holds up when you contact them through a way you already had. The details are the part of that which the email does not satisfy: they are new, and they came in a message. Everything else about the case would fit a real request, and that is exactly why the copy works.

It is the question from Tessa’s two invoices. Are the bank account details the ones I was given at the start, or has a message just told me that they have changed? Here the account has changed, so the answer is **“Pay into new bank account details sent by message”**. Whether the change is real is not something that the email can tell her. She finds that out by calling the supplier at a number from the contract.

**Does it look like a case you know?**

You have your answer. Now take a second look of a different kind: does this case look like one you know? It should bring back the landscaper’s email: the usual thread, the usual signature, and one line about a new bank.

Here the questions and the likeness agree, so the answer stands. The questions come first, because they make you point at words in the case. The likeness is only a second look. When the two disagree, do not pick the one you prefer. Go back to the questions and find the words in the case that answer them. The third whole case shows how.

### 58. A second whole case, where the request is real

*Unit Four · rev 3 · Draft: not yet read by a newcomer · Part 5 of 5 · Card 58 of 62*

[reviewers only: card kind `worked`, id `worked-cottage`]

The compost supplier was a copy. Not every case is. In this one the request is real, and the questions are asked in exactly the same way.

*The cottage deposit*

> Ruth booked a weekend cottage through the owner's own website, which she found by asking a friend and typing in the address. The booking email in her inbox says: 'Please pay the $200 deposit by May 20, into the account named on your booking confirmation. The balance is due on arrival. Call the owner at the number on the website if you would like to talk about it.' The deposit and the balance are the figures on the website.

**Question 1 of 3: What is it asking you to do right now?**

What it is for: sorts a message, a call or an offer by the next thing it asks of you: something on your device, a way into an account, money, facts about you, or nothing at all.

> Ruth booked a weekend cottage through the owner's own website, which she found by asking a friend and typing in the address. The booking email in her inbox says: '⟦Please pay the $200 deposit by May 20⟧, into the account named on your booking confirmation. The balance is due on arrival. Call the owner at the number on the website if you would like to talk about it.' The deposit and the balance are the figures on the website.

Answer: **“Pay or send money”**

The email asks Ruth to pay a deposit: “Please pay the $200 deposit by May 20”. Nothing earlier in the list is asked of her, so the answer is **“Pay or send money”**.

Still possible: all nine names this unit teaches.

**Question 2 of 3: What does the request say the money is for?**

What it is for: sorts requests for money by the reason they give, which leaves one name or a few.

> ⟦Ruth booked a weekend cottage through the owner's own website⟧, which she found by asking a friend and typing in the address. The booking email in her inbox says: 'Please pay the $200 deposit by May 20, into the account named on your booking confirmation. The balance is due on arrival. Call the owner at the number on the website if you would like to talk about it.' The deposit and the balance are the figures on the website.

Answer: **“Something you are buying, selling or booking”**

The deposit is part of a booking that she made herself: “Ruth booked a weekend cottage through the owner's own website”. That is a deal that she is in, so the answer is **“Something you are buying, selling or booking”**.

Still possible: **Real payment request**, **Fake payment link** and **Overpayment scam**. Ruled out: **Romance scam**, **Pig-butchering scam**, **Advance-fee scam**, **Recovery scam**, **Invoice fraud** and **Fake official scam**.

**Question 3 of 3: What does it ask you to do with the money?**

What it is for: tells apart the names that share a reason for paying, by what the request asks you to do with the money.

> Ruth booked a weekend cottage through the owner's own website, which she found by asking a friend and typing in the address. The booking email in her inbox says: 'Please pay the $200 deposit by May 20, ⟦into the account named on your booking confirmation⟧. The balance is due on arrival. Call the owner at the number on the website if you would like to talk about it.' ⟦The deposit and the balance are the figures on the website⟧.

Answer: **“Pay what you agreed or owe, to details that pass the check”**

She has checked what she can without anyone’s help. The deposit is to go to the account named on her booking confirmation, which she already had, and the figures are the ones on the website that she found herself: “into the account named on your booking confirmation” and “The deposit and the balance are the figures on the website”. The email also invites her to call the owner. Nobody hurries her and nobody asks her to keep it quiet. So the answer is **“Pay what you agreed or owe, to details that pass the check”**.

Still possible: **Real payment request**. Ruled out: **Romance scam**, **Pig-butchering scam**, **Advance-fee scam**, **Recovery scam**, **Invoice fraud**, **Fake payment link**, **Fake official scam** and **Overpayment scam**.

**Name it:** **Real payment request**

**You are asked:** The email gives bank account details to pay into, and bank account details in an email are what the first whole case turned on. Why is this **Real payment request** and not **Invoice fraud**? Every statement below is true of the case. Before you read the reason, choose the one that settles it.

- (a) It asks her to pay money into a bank account.
- (b) The account is the one named on her booking confirmation, which she already had, and nothing in the email says that anything has changed.
- (c) It comes from the owner whose website she visited.

**Shown as soon as you choose.** The one that settles it is (b): The account is the one named on her booking confirmation, which she already had, and nothing in the email says that anything has changed.
- If you chose (a): True, and it is why the case can look like **Invoice fraud**. But a real request and a copy both ask for money to go into an account, so this does not separate them.
- If you chose (c): True, but a copy can use the sender’s name as well, so it does not settle which of the two this is.

**Why this is Real payment request and not Invoice fraud**

For **Invoice fraud** you must be able to point to this: a bill or a payment you already make to someone, a message saying that their bank account details have changed, and new details to pay into. There are no new details here. The account is the one that she was given when she booked, so the one thing that the copy is made of is missing.

It is the question from Tessa’s two invoices. Are the bank account details the ones I was given at the start, or has a message just told me that they have changed? Here nothing has changed, so the answer is **“Pay what you agreed or owe, to details that pass the check”**, and the case is **Real payment request**. A real request is as much an answer as a scam is, and saying so is what the questions are for.

**Does it look like a case you know?**

Now take a second look: does this case look like one you know? It should bring back Hana’s rent reminder: a payment that is due, an account that she was given at the start, and a number to call.

Here the questions and the likeness agree, so the answer stands. Notice that the likeness points to the real thing as readily as to a copy. That is the reason the questions come first.

### 59. A third whole case, where the story points the wrong way

*Unit Four · rev 3 · Draft: not yet read by a newcomer · Part 5 of 5 · Card 59 of 62*

[reviewers only: card kind `worked`, id `worked-sofa`]

The first case was clean, and the second was real. In this third case the most noticeable thing in the story is not the thing that decides it. Watch which words each question picks out.

*The sofa and the secret*

> Imogen advertises a sofa for $350. A buyer pays $700 by Zelle and writes at once: 'Sorry, my mistake! I need the $350 back today, by transfer to my sister's account, and please do not tell your bank about the extra, as it will freeze both our accounts.'

**Question 1 of 3: What is it asking you to do right now?**

What it is for: sorts a message, a call or an offer by the next thing it asks of you: something on your device, a way into an account, money, facts about you, or nothing at all.

> Imogen advertises a sofa for $350. A buyer pays $700 by Zelle and writes at once: 'Sorry, my mistake! ⟦I need the $350 back today, by transfer to my sister's account⟧, and please do not tell your bank about the extra, as it will freeze both our accounts.'

Answer: **“Pay or send money”**

The buyer asks Imogen to send money: “I need the $350 back today, by transfer to my sister's account”. Nothing earlier in the list is asked of her, so the answer is **“Pay or send money”**.

Still possible: all nine names this unit teaches.

**Question 2 of 3: What does the request say the money is for?**

What it is for: sorts requests for money by the reason they give, which leaves one name or a few.

> ⟦Imogen advertises a sofa for $350⟧. A buyer pays $700 by Zelle and writes at once: 'Sorry, my mistake! I need the $350 back today, by transfer to my sister's account, and please do not tell your bank about the extra, as it will freeze both our accounts.'

Answer: **“Something you are buying, selling or booking”**

The money is part of a sale that she is making: “Imogen advertises a sofa for $350”. That is a deal she is in, so the answer is **“Something you are buying, selling or booking”**. There is no official, no prize, no loss and no stranger who showed her a site. The person behind the request is a buyer.

Still possible: **Real payment request**, **Fake payment link** and **Overpayment scam**. Ruled out: **Romance scam**, **Pig-butchering scam**, **Advance-fee scam**, **Recovery scam**, **Invoice fraud** and **Fake official scam**.

**Question 3 of 3: What does it ask you to do with the money?**

What it is for: tells apart the names that share a reason for paying, by what the request asks you to do with the money.

> Imogen advertises a sofa for $350. A buyer pays $700 by Zelle and writes at once: '⟦Sorry, my mistake! I need the $350 back today, by transfer to my sister's account⟧, and please do not tell your bank about the extra, as it will freeze both our accounts.'

Answer: **“Send back money they say they paid you by mistake”**

This is the question that matters. The buyer has paid twice the price and asks her to send the extra to someone else’s account: “Sorry, my mistake! I need the $350 back today, by transfer to my sister's account”. That is the answer **“Send back money they say they paid you by mistake”**. The text also says today, by transfer, and do not tell your bank. Those words are a hurry, a payment that cannot be undone, and a secret, which is what the answer **“Pay at once, in a way that cannot be undone, and tell no one”** sounds like. When a request shows both, the answer is the more specific one, and the more specific one is the payment to send back.

Still possible: **Overpayment scam**. Ruled out: **Romance scam**, **Pig-butchering scam**, **Advance-fee scam**, **Recovery scam**, **Real payment request**, **Invoice fraud**, **Fake payment link** and **Fake official scam**.

**Name it:** **Overpayment scam**

**You are asked:** The text is hurried and secret and about a payment that cannot be undone, which is how the tax caller sounded. Why is this **Overpayment scam** and not **Fake official scam**? Every statement below is true of the case. Before you read the reason, choose the one that settles it.

- (a) It says to send the money today, by transfer, and not to tell the bank.
- (b) It says that the buyer paid twice the price, and asks for the extra to be sent to someone else’s account.
- (c) It comes from a buyer, and not from an official or a bank.

**Shown as soon as you choose.** The one that settles it is (b): It says that the buyer paid twice the price, and asks for the extra to be sent to someone else’s account.
- If you chose (a): True, and it is why the case can look like **Fake official scam**. But hurry and secrecy turn up in many scams, so they cannot settle which of the two this is.
- If you chose (c): True, and it is what the first question settles. It does not say what the request asks you to do with the money, which is the question that separates these two.

**Why this is Overpayment scam and not Fake official scam**

For **Fake official scam** you must be able to point to this: someone who says they are an official or from your bank, a fine, a debt or a danger to your money, and an order to pay at once by gift cards, crypto, cash or a transfer to an account they give you, often with an order to tell no one. There is no official here, and no fine, debt or danger. The sender is a buyer, and the money is part of a sale. A hurry and a secret are there, but they are only part of what that name needs.

The question that separates the two is the one from the first question card. Is the money part of a deal that I am in, with a payment that has reached me, or does someone with an official’s power threaten me unless I pay? Here the money is part of a deal that Imogen is in, and the buyer asks her to send some back. So the answer is **“Send back money they say they paid you by mistake”**, and the name is **Overpayment scam**.

**Does it look like a case you know?**

Now the second look: does this case look like one you know? A buyer who hurries you, tells you to keep a payment from your bank and wants a transfer today may bring back the tax caller first, and that case was **Fake official scam**. So here the likeness and the questions seem to disagree.

When that happens, go back to the questions and find the words in the case that answer them. They are “Sorry, my mistake! I need the $350 back today, by transfer to my sister's account”. The tax caller has nothing like them: there was no deal and no payment from the other side. The bike with a typo does: someone had paid twice the price, and asked for the difference to be sent on. So the case this one really looks like is Rafa’s bike, and the answer stands.

### The drill

The cards are out of view from here, and every case is new. The drill has five stages. Cases that are easy to mix up are placed next to each other on purpose. This is meant to feel harder than the questions between the cards: telling look-alikes apart side by side is what makes the difference stick. Five of the cases come from an earlier unit, without being labeled. Nothing here is graded. A miss only decides what comes back. What you miss is asked again before the drill ends, and every name comes back on later days with a new case.

Some of these requests are real, and some are copies made to take money. That is on purpose. Nothing in the questions says that a request is a scam. They ask what it says the money is for and what it asks you to do with it, and a real request gets a name of its own, so you will need that name as often as the others. When the name is a scam, it also tells you what to do next.

#### Stage 1 of 5. The answers are shown for each case. Give the name that goes with them. This stage practices one thing: which name goes with which answer.

The names offered are the nine this unit teaches: Romance scam / Pig-butchering scam / Advance-fee scam / Recovery scam / Real payment request / Invoice fraud / Fake payment link / Fake official scam / Overpayment scam.

**Drill item 1 of 85**

> Carol has written every day for six months to a man called Mike, who says he is a soldier posted overseas. She has never seen him on a live call. He writes: 'I was stopped for a traffic violation, and ⟦the court will release me only if I pay a $2,400 fine⟧. ⟦Please send it to my lawyer's account⟧.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:
- What is it asking you to do right now? **Pay or send money**
- What does the request say the money is for? **An investment or an emergency of someone you know only online**
- What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay for an emergency of someone you have never met**

**You are asked:** Which name goes with these answers?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Romance scam**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay for an emergency of someone you have never met.** The request is to pay for his emergency, a fine that keeps him in custody: “the court will release me only if I pay a $2,400 fine”. Nothing is to be invested.
  - Why not **Pig-butchering scam**: Mike does not ask her to put money into any site or app. The money is for his own trouble.
- If you miss, you see the right name, the reason and the “why not” line above, and then:
  - If you chose **Pig-butchering scam**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both begin with someone you have only ever known through messages, often for weeks or months, and both end in a request for a large sum. The same person often runs one after the other. **Romance scam** asks you to pay for trouble that the person says is their own, such as a hospital bill or a ticket home. **Pig-butchering scam** asks you to invest: to put your money into a platform that the person showed you, or to pay a charge to that platform before your money can come out. Is the money to pay for something that has gone wrong for the person, or is it to be invested in a platform that they pointed me to?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”
- Taught on: “An online partner’s emergency that needs your money” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 2 of 85**

> Sofia has chatted with a man called Jin in an online game for three months. ⟦He tells her that he trades on a currency app that his cousin set up, and sends her a link to it⟧. '⟦Put $1,500 in this week⟧,' he writes, 'and I will show you how to take the profit.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:
- What is it asking you to do right now? **Pay or send money**
- What does the request say the money is for? **An investment or an emergency of someone you know only online**
- What does it ask you to do with the money? **Put it into a trading site or app that they showed you**

**You are asked:** Which name goes with these answers?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Pig-butchering scam**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Put it into a trading site or app that they showed you.** She is asked to put money into the app that he showed her: “Put $1,500 in this week”. It is not for any trouble of his own.
  - Why not **Romance scam**: Jin does not have an emergency. The money is to go into an app that he showed her, not to pay for his trouble.
- If you miss, you see the right name, the reason and the “why not” line above, and then:
  - If you chose **Romance scam**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both begin with someone you have only ever known through messages, often for weeks or months, and both end in a request for a large sum. The same person often runs one after the other. **Romance scam** asks you to pay for trouble that the person says is their own, such as a hospital bill or a ticket home. **Pig-butchering scam** asks you to invest: to put your money into a platform that the person showed you, or to pay a charge to that platform before your money can come out. Is the money to pay for something that has gone wrong for the person, or is it to be invested in a platform that they pointed me to?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”
- Taught on: “An online friend’s investment site that keeps your money” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 3 of 85**

> An attorney's email tells Brian that ⟦a man he has never heard of has died and left him $420,000⟧. 'The estate can be paid out ⟦as soon as you have sent $1,100 for the transfer certificate to the account below⟧.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:
- What is it asking you to do right now? **Pay or send money**
- What does the request say the money is for? **A prize, an inheritance, a grant or a loan waiting for you**
- What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay a fee before the money reaches you**

**You are asked:** Which name goes with these answers?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Advance-fee scam**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay a fee before the money reaches you.** He must pay before any of it reaches him: “as soon as you have sent $1,100 for the transfer certificate”. The fee comes first.
  - Why not **Recovery scam**: The $420,000 was never Brian’s and nothing was taken from him, so this is not an offer to get back something he lost.
- If you miss, you see the right name, the reason and the “why not” line above, and then:
  - If you chose **Recovery scam**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both are about money that is said to be waiting to be released to you, and both ask for a fee before you can have it. In **Advance-fee scam** the money was never yours: a prize, a grant, a loan or an inheritance. In **Recovery scam** the money was yours and was taken from you earlier, and someone offers to get it back. Is the money that is said to be waiting something that was never mine, or something that I lost earlier?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”
- Taught on: “A fee to collect money that is not coming” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 4 of 85**

> Two years after she paid $12,000 to a man she met online, Lily gets a call at work from a woman who says she is from a 'victim support unit': '⟦We have found your money⟧. ⟦Our fee to release it is $800, to be paid by wire transfer before we start⟧.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:
- What is it asking you to do right now? **Pay or send money**
- What does the request say the money is for? **Getting back money you lost**
- What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay a fee before the money reaches you**

**You are asked:** Which name goes with these answers?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Recovery scam**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay a fee before the money reaches you.** There is a fee that comes first: “Our fee to release it is $800, to be paid by wire transfer before we start”.
  - Why not **Advance-fee scam**: The money is not a prize or a grant that was never hers. It is money that she paid and lost.
- If you miss, you see the right name, the reason and the “why not” line above, and then:
  - If you chose **Advance-fee scam**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both are about money that is said to be waiting to be released to you, and both ask for a fee before you can have it. In **Advance-fee scam** the money was never yours: a prize, a grant, a loan or an inheritance. In **Recovery scam** the money was yours and was taken from you earlier, and someone offers to get it back. Is the money that is said to be waiting something that was never mine, or something that I lost earlier?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”
- Taught on: “A fee to get back money you lost” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 5 of 85**

> Moses asked a plumber to fix his furnace, and accepted the written quote of $340. After the job ⟦an invoice arrives from the plumber's own address, with the same figure⟧ and ⟦the bank account details that were on the quote⟧. It says: '⟦Please pay within 14 days⟧. ⟦Call the number on my quote⟧ if you want to talk about it.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:
- What is it asking you to do right now? **Pay or send money**
- What does the request say the money is for? **A bill from someone you already pay**
- What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay what you agreed or owe, to details that pass the check**

**You are asked:** Which name goes with these answers?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Real payment request**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay what you agreed or owe, to details that pass the check.** Nothing has changed since he agreed. The details match the ones he was given, and he is invited to contact the plumber at the number he already had: “the bank account details that were on the quote” and “Call the number on my quote”.
  - Why not **Invoice fraud**: The bank account details are the ones on the quote. Nothing in the email says that they have changed.
- If you miss, you see the right name, the reason and the “why not” line above, and then:
  - If you chose **Invoice fraud**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both are a bill that you really pay, with the same logo, the same email thread and the same amount. In **Real payment request** the account to pay into is the one that you have always paid, or were given when you started. In **Invoice fraud** a message tells you that the details have changed, and gives new ones. Are the bank account details the ones I was given at the start, or has a message just told me that they have changed?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”
- Taught on: “A bill, a fine or a deal that is what it says” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 6 of 85**

> Mr. Hale looks after a school's accounts, and ⟦pays a catering firm every month⟧. An email from the firm's usual address says: '⟦We now bank with a different bank⟧. ⟦Please pay April's invoice of $4,300 into the account below⟧.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:
- What is it asking you to do right now? **Pay or send money**
- What does the request say the money is for? **A bill from someone you already pay**
- What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay into new bank account details sent by message**

**You are asked:** Which name goes with these answers?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Invoice fraud**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay into new bank account details sent by message.** A message tells him that the bank has changed: “We now bank with a different bank”. The new details are what he is asked to pay into.
  - Why not **Real payment request**: The amount and the sender are what he expects, but the details are new, and they arrived in a message. A real request uses the details he was given at the start.
- If you miss, you see the right name, the reason and the “why not” line above, and then:
  - If you chose **Real payment request**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both are a bill that you really pay, with the same logo, the same email thread and the same amount. In **Real payment request** the account to pay into is the one that you have always paid, or were given when you started. In **Invoice fraud** a message tells you that the details have changed, and gives new ones. Are the bank account details the ones I was given at the start, or has a message just told me that they have changed?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”
- Taught on: “A real bill, sent to new bank account details” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 7 of 85**

> Karin has ordered something this week. A text from a number she does not know says: '⟦We tried to deliver your order today⟧. ⟦Re-book a time and pay a $1.45 fee at fastdrop-rebook.example⟧.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:
- What is it asking you to do right now? **Pay or send money**
- What does the request say the money is for? **Something you are buying, selling or booking**
- What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay on a page reached from a link in the message**

**You are asked:** Which name goes with these answers?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Fake payment link**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay on a page reached from a link in the message.** She is told to pay on a page that she reaches through a link in the text: “Re-book a time and pay a $1.45 fee at fastdrop-rebook.example”.
  - Why not **Real payment request**: Nothing that she already had shows that the charge is real. It came in a message, with a link to pay on.
- If you miss, you see the right name, the reason and the “why not” line above, and then:
  - If you chose **Real payment request**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both are a small charge on something that you are waiting for or already pay for, from a company that you deal with, and both can lead to a payment page. In **Real payment request** you find the charge yourself, in the company’s own app or on a page that you already use, and pay it there. In **Fake payment link** the charge arrives in a message, and the page to pay on is behind a link in that message. Did the charge come to me in a message with a link to pay on, or can I find it myself in the company’s own app or on a page that I already use?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”
- Taught on: “A small charge you do not owe, paid through a link” (one tap opens the card).

> A man calls Raj and says that he is from immigration: '⟦Your visa has been canceled, and the police will collect you tomorrow⟧ unless you ⟦clear $1,800 now⟧. ⟦Buy gift cards, read me the numbers, and tell nobody⟧.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:

- What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay at once, in a way that cannot be undone, and tell no one**

**You are asked:** Which name goes with these answers?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Fake official scam**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay at once, in a way that cannot be undone, and tell no one.** Raj is to pay at once, in a way that cannot be undone, and to tell nobody: “Buy gift cards, read me the numbers, and tell nobody”. Nothing more specific shows.
  - Why not **Fake payment link**: There is no link and no payment page. Raj is told to buy gift cards and read out the numbers.
- If you miss, you see the right name, the reason and the “why not” line above, and then:
  - If you chose **Fake payment link**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both can come from someone who says that they are an official, with a fine or a debt, a deadline and a threat. In **Fake payment link** the request is to pay on a page that you reach through a link in the message, and a hurry or a threat does not change that. In **Fake official scam** nothing more specific shows: you are told to pay at once, in a way that cannot be undone, and to tell no one. Am I told to pay on a page that I reach through a link, or told to pay at once in a way that cannot be undone, with nothing more specific?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”

In **Overpayment scam** a payment arrives that is more than the price, and you are asked to send some of it back or on. Is the amount the one that was agreed, or am I being asked to send some of a payment back or on to someone else?

#### Stage 2 of 5. One question at a time.

**Drill item 12 of 85**

> Hamid has messaged every evening for five months with a woman called Rina, whom he met on a hiking forum. She writes: 'My card was blocked in Dubai and the hotel has kept my passport until I pay $1,950. Please send it to this account.'

**You are asked:** What does the request say the money is for?

- An investment or an emergency of someone you know only online
- A prize, an inheritance, a grant or a loan waiting for you
- Getting back money you lost
- A bill from someone you already pay
- A fine, a tax, or keeping your money safe
- Something you are buying, selling or booking

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **An investment or an emergency of someone you know only online.**” Rina is someone he knows only from a forum, and the money is for trouble that she says is hers: “My card was blocked in Dubai and the hotel has kept my passport until I pay $1,950”. This answer leads to **Pig-butchering scam** and **Romance scam**.
- If you miss: “The answer is …”, the same reason, and then a line about the answer you chose:
  - If you chose another answer, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “You chose «that answer». Give that answer when «what a case must show for it». This case shows something else: «what a case must show for the right answer».”
- Taught on: “The first question about money: what is it for?” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 13 of 85**

> Rosa paid $4,000 for a used motorcycle online, and it never arrived. A message comes from a 'chargeback specialist': 'We can get your $4,000 back from the seller's bank. Pay our $300 case fee first.'

- If you miss: “The answer is …”, the same reason, and then a line about the answer you chose:
  - If you chose **A prize, an inheritance, a grant or a loan waiting for you**: The $4,000 is not a prize or a grant that was never hers. It is money that she paid and lost.
  - If you chose any other answer, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “You chose «that answer». Give that answer when «what a case must show for it». This case shows something else: «what a case must show for the right answer».”

**Drill item 23 of 85**

**You are asked:** You cannot decide whether a case is **Pig-butchering scam** or **Romance scam**. Which question do you put to the case?

- Is the money to pay for something that has gone wrong for the person, or is it to be invested in a platform that they pointed me to?
- Did someone I know only online show me a site or an app where my money is, or is the money said to be waiting for me from someone who contacted me?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: “Is the money to pay for something that has gone wrong for the person, or is it to be invested in a platform that they pointed me to?” Both begin with someone you have only ever known through messages, often for weeks or months, and both end in a request for a large sum. The same person often runs one after the other. **Romance scam** asks you to pay for trouble that the person says is their own, such as a hospital bill or a ticket home. **Pig-butchering scam** asks you to invest: to put your money into a platform that the person showed you, or to pay a charge to that platform before your money can come out.
- If you chose “Did someone I know only online show me a site or an app where my money is, or is the money said to be waiting for me from someone who contacted me?”: that question separates **Pig-butchering scam** and **Advance-fee scam**.
- Taught on: “Pig-butchering scam or Romance scam: telling them apart” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 24 of 85**

**You are asked:** You cannot decide whether a case is **Advance-fee scam** or **Recovery scam**. Which question do you put to the case?

- Is the money that is said to be waiting something that was never mine, or something that I lost earlier?
- Did someone I know only online show me a site or an app where my money is, or is the money said to be waiting for me from someone who contacted me?
- Does the message say that money is owed to me and that I must pay first, or that I owe money and will be punished unless I pay?
- Is the payment a fee to receive something that is said to be waiting for me, or a charge on something that I am buying or already pay for?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: “Is the money that is said to be waiting something that was never mine, or something that I lost earlier?” Both are about money that is said to be waiting to be released to you, and both ask for a fee before you can have it. In **Advance-fee scam** the money was never yours: a prize, a grant, a loan or an inheritance. In **Recovery scam** the money was yours and was taken from you earlier, and someone offers to get it back. When a case shows both **“A prize, an inheritance, a grant or a loan waiting for you”** and money you lost earlier, which someone says they can get back, the answer is **“Getting back money you lost”**.
- If you chose “Did someone I know only online show me a site or an app where my money is, or is the money said to be waiting for me from someone who contacted me?”: that question separates **Pig-butchering scam** and **Advance-fee scam**.
- If you chose “Does the message say that money is owed to me and that I must pay first, or that I owe money and will be punished unless I pay?”: that question separates **Advance-fee scam** and **Fake official scam**.
- If you chose “Is the payment a fee to receive something that is said to be waiting for me, or a charge on something that I am buying or already pay for?”: that question separates **Advance-fee scam** and **Fake payment link**.
- Taught on: “Advance-fee scam or Recovery scam: telling them apart” (one tap opens the card).

In **Invoice fraud** a message tells you that the details have changed, and gives new ones.

**Shown as soon as you answer**

In **Real payment request** you are given time and a way to appeal, you can look the amount up yourself, and you are asked to pay in an ordinary way. In **Fake official scam** you are told to pay at once, in a way that cannot be undone, and to say nothing.

- If you chose “Am I asked to read out or type in a code that has just been sent to me, or to move or send money?”: that question separates **Fake official scam** and **One-time code scam**.
- If you chose “Is the money part of a deal that I am in, with a payment that has reached me, or does someone with an official’s power threaten me unless I pay?”: that question separates **Fake official scam** and **Overpayment scam**.
- Taught on: “Fake official scam or Real payment request: telling them apart” (one tap opens the card).

In **Overpayment scam** a payment arrives that is more than the price, and you are asked to send some of it back or on.
- If you chose “Are the bank account details the ones I was given at the start, or has a message just told me that they have changed?”: that question separates **Invoice fraud** and **Real payment request**.

- If you chose “Has money reached me first, so that I am asked to send some of it back, or am I asked to pay on a page that I reach through a link?”: that question separates **Overpayment scam** and **Fake payment link**.
- If you chose “Before any money is sent, am I asked to install something or to let someone watch my phone or computer, or only to send the money?”: that question separates **Overpayment scam** and **Refund scam**.
- If you chose “Is the money part of a deal that I am in, with a payment that has reached me, or does someone with an official’s power threaten me unless I pay?”: that question separates **Fake official scam** and **Overpayment scam**.

**Drill item 30 of 85**

**You are asked:** You cannot decide whether a case is **Pig-butchering scam** or **Advance-fee scam**. Which question do you put to the case?

- Is the money to pay for something that has gone wrong for the person, or is it to be invested in a platform that they pointed me to?
- Is the money that is said to be waiting something that was never mine, or something that I lost earlier?
- Did someone I know only online show me a site or an app where my money is, or is the money said to be waiting for me from someone who contacted me?
- Does the message say that money is owed to me and that I must pay first, or that I owe money and will be punished unless I pay?
- Is the payment a fee to receive something that is said to be waiting for me, or a charge on something that I am buying or already pay for?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: “Did someone I know only online show me a site or an app where my money is, or is the money said to be waiting for me from someone who contacted me?” Both ask for a fee before money reaches you or leaves a site, and both can promise large sums. In **Pig-butchering scam** the money is in a trading site or app that someone you know only online showed you, and the fee is to take it out. In **Advance-fee scam** the money is said to be waiting for you from someone who contacted you, and you never put any in.
- If you chose “Is the money to pay for something that has gone wrong for the person, or is it to be invested in a platform that they pointed me to?”: that question separates **Pig-butchering scam** and **Romance scam**.
- If you chose “Is the money that is said to be waiting something that was never mine, or something that I lost earlier?”: that question separates **Advance-fee scam** and **Recovery scam**.
- If you chose “Does the message say that money is owed to me and that I must pay first, or that I owe money and will be punished unless I pay?”: that question separates **Advance-fee scam** and **Fake official scam**.
- If you chose “Is the payment a fee to receive something that is said to be waiting for me, or a charge on something that I am buying or already pay for?”: that question separates **Advance-fee scam** and **Fake payment link**.
- Taught on: “A tax to take your profit out” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 31 of 85**

**You are asked:** You cannot tell whether a case is **Pig-butchering scam** or **Romance scam**. Which question tells these two apart?

- What does the request say the money is for?
- What does it ask you to do with the money?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: **What does it ask you to do with the money?** Both begin with someone you have only ever known through messages, often for weeks or months, and both end in a request for a large sum. The same person often runs one after the other. **Romance scam** asks you to pay for trouble that the person says is their own, such as a hospital bill or a ticket home. **Pig-butchering scam** asks you to invest: to put your money into a platform that the person showed you, or to pay a charge to that platform before your money can come out. **Pig-butchering scam**: Put it into a trading site or app that they showed you. **Romance scam**: Pay for an emergency of someone you have never met.
- If you chose “What does the request say the money is for?”: “You chose that question. Both of these give the answer An investment or an emergency of someone you know only online, so that question does not separate them.”
- Taught on: “The second question about money: what does it ask you to do with it?” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 32 of 85**

**You are asked:** You cannot tell whether a case is **Advance-fee scam** or **Recovery scam**. Which question tells these two apart?

- What does the request say the money is for?
- What does it ask you to do with the money?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: **What does the request say the money is for?** Both are about money that is said to be waiting to be released to you, and both ask for a fee before you can have it. In **Advance-fee scam** the money was never yours: a prize, a grant, a loan or an inheritance. In **Recovery scam** the money was yours and was taken from you earlier, and someone offers to get it back. **Advance-fee scam**: A prize, an inheritance, a grant or a loan waiting for you. **Recovery scam**: Getting back money you lost.
- If you chose “What does it ask you to do with the money?”: “You chose that question. Both of these give the answer Pay a fee before the money reaches you, so that question does not separate them.”
- Taught on: “The first question about money: what is it for?” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 33 of 85**

**You are asked:** You cannot tell whether a case is **Fake payment link** or **Real payment request**. Which question tells these two apart?

- What does the request say the money is for?
- What does it ask you to do with the money?

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: **What does it ask you to do with the money?** Both are a small charge on something that you are waiting for or already pay for, from a company that you deal with, and both can lead to a payment page. In **Real payment request** you find the charge yourself, in the company’s own app or on a page that you already use, and pay it there. In **Fake payment link** the charge arrives in a message, and the page to pay on is behind a link in that message. **Fake payment link**: Pay on a page reached from a link in the message. **Real payment request**: Pay what you agreed or owe, to details that pass the check.
- If you chose “What does the request say the money is for?”: “You chose that question. Both of these give the answer A bill from someone you already pay and A fine, a tax, or keeping your money safe and Something you are buying, selling or booking, so that question does not separate them.”
- Taught on: “The second question about money: what does it ask you to do with it?” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 34 of 85**

**You are asked:** This is **Romance scam**. Which of these would you expect to hear?

- "My bag was stolen and I cannot get home. Could you send $900 for a ticket?"
- "Open an account on the platform with $3,000, and I will guide you."
- "You have won $250,000. Pay the handling fee to collect it."
- "Please disregard our old bank account details and pay into this account."

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: "My bag was stolen and I cannot get home. Could you send $900 for a ticket?" Someone you have never met who asks for money for an emergency of their own sounds like a ticket home, a hospital bill or a blocked card. The trading platform, the prize and the changed account belong to other names.
- If you chose "Open an account on the platform with $3,000, and I will guide you.": that belongs to **Pig-butchering scam**.
- If you chose "You have won $250,000. Pay the handling fee to collect it.": that belongs to **Advance-fee scam**.
- If you chose "Please disregard our old bank account details and pay into this account.": that belongs to **Invoice fraud**.
- Taught on: “Romance scam: what it is like” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 35 of 85**

**You are asked:** This is **Pig-butchering scam**. Which of these would you expect to hear?

- "My uncle runs the platform. Put in $3,000, and your profits will triple."
- "We have traced the money you lost. A release fee will unlock it."
- "I would not ask if I had anyone else. Please send $1,100."
- "Pay the $2.99 redelivery fee at this address."

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: "My uncle runs the platform. Put in $3,000, and your profits will triple." The money is to go into a site or an app that a person you know only online showed you, and the talk is of profits. A release fee, a personal emergency and a package fee belong to other names.
- If you chose "We have traced the money you lost. A release fee will unlock it.": that belongs to **Recovery scam**.
- If you chose "I would not ask if I had anyone else. Please send $1,100.": that belongs to **Romance scam**.
- If you chose "Pay the $2.99 redelivery fee at this address.": that belongs to **Fake payment link**.
- Taught on: “Pig-butchering scam: what it is like” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 36 of 85**

**You are asked:** This is **Advance-fee scam**. Which detail would you expect to find in the case?

- A prize, a loan or an inheritance that is said to be waiting, and a fee to pay before it is released.
- A person you have never met who asks for money for their own emergency.
- A bill with the usual account, and a number to call if you want to be sure.
- A caller who threatens you with arrest unless you pay at once.

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: A prize, a loan or an inheritance that is said to be waiting, and a fee to pay before it is released. Money that is said to be waiting for you, and a fee that comes first, is what you would find. An emergency, a usual bill and a threat belong to other names.
- If you chose “A person you have never met who asks for money for their own emergency.”: that belongs to **Romance scam**.
- If you chose “A bill with the usual account, and a number to call if you want to be sure.”: that belongs to **Real payment request**.
- If you chose “A caller who threatens you with arrest unless you pay at once.”: that belongs to **Fake official scam**.
- Taught on: “Advance-fee scam: what it is like” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 37 of 85**

**You are asked:** This is **Recovery scam**. Which of these would you expect to hear?

- "We have traced the money you lost. Pay the retainer, and we start."
- "Your loan is approved. Pay the insurance deposit to release it."
- "Pay the $2.99 customs charge at this link."
- "Sorry, I typed an extra zero. Send the difference to my courier."

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: "We have traced the money you lost. Pay the retainer, and we start." An offer to get back money that you lost, with a fee first, is what you would hear. A loan that is approved is money that was never yours, which is the other name.
- If you chose "Your loan is approved. Pay the insurance deposit to release it.": that belongs to **Advance-fee scam**.
- If you chose "Pay the $2.99 customs charge at this link.": that belongs to **Fake payment link**.
- If you chose "Sorry, I typed an extra zero. Send the difference to my courier.": that belongs to **Overpayment scam**.

**Drill item 38 of 85**

**You are asked:** This is **Invoice fraud**. Which detail would you expect to find in the case?

- A bill from someone you already pay, and a message that says the bank account details have changed.
- A payment that is more than the price, and a request to send some of it back.
- A prize that you never entered, and a fee to claim it.
- The same account that you have paid for years, and an invitation to call if anything looks wrong.

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: A bill from someone you already pay, and a message that says the bank account details have changed. A bill that you really pay, with a message announcing new details to pay into, is what you would find. The same account as always, with an invitation to check, is the real request.
- If you chose “A payment that is more than the price, and a request to send some of it back.”: that belongs to **Overpayment scam**.
- If you chose “A prize that you never entered, and a fee to claim it.”: that belongs to **Advance-fee scam**.
- If you chose “The same account that you have paid for years, and an invitation to call if anything looks wrong.”: that belongs to **Real payment request**.
- Taught on: “Invoice fraud: what it is like” (one tap opens the card).

- "Your package is held. Pay a $2 fee at this address."
- "Open an account with $2,000, and I will guide you."

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: "A warrant has been issued. Buy gift cards, read me the numbers and tell no one." A threat from someone with an official’s power, with a payment that is at once, final and secret, is what you would hear. An invoice, a package fee and an account to open belong to other names.
- If you chose "Your invoice is attached, to the same account as always.": that belongs to **Real payment request**.
- If you chose "Your package is held. Pay a $2 fee at this address.": that belongs to **Fake payment link**.

- A caller who stays on the line while you buy gift cards.
- A friend you have never met who asks you to pay a hospital bill.
- A buyer who pays twice the price and asks for the extra to be sent on.

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: A small charge on a delivery, a toll or a subscription, and a link to a payment page that asks for your card. A small charge with a link to a payment page that wants your card is what you would find. Gift cards over the phone, a hospital bill and an overpaying buyer belong to other names.
- If you chose “A caller who stays on the line while you buy gift cards.”: that belongs to **Fake official scam**.
- If you chose “A friend you have never met who asks you to pay a hospital bill.”: that belongs to **Romance scam**.
- If you chose “A buyer who pays twice the price and asks for the extra to be sent on.”: that belongs to **Overpayment scam**.
- Taught on: “Fake payment link: what it is like” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 41 of 85**

**You are asked:** This is **Overpayment scam**. Which of these would you expect to hear?

- "Sorry, my mistake. Please send the extra $400 to my courier."
- "Rent is due on the 1st, to the account in your agreement."
- "Do not tell the bank. They may be involved."
- "A release fee will unlock the money we have recovered."

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- The answer is: "Sorry, my mistake. Please send the extra $400 to my courier." A buyer who says that a payment was a slip, and asks you to send the difference on, is what you would hear. A rent reminder, a secret and a release fee belong to other names.
- If you chose "Rent is due on the 1st, to the account in your agreement.": that belongs to **Real payment request**.
- If you chose "Do not tell the bank. They may be involved.": that belongs to **Fake official scam**.
- If you chose "A release fee will unlock the money we have recovered.": that belongs to **Recovery scam**.
- Taught on: “Overpayment scam: what it is like” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 45 of 85**

> Joan has played chess online with a man called Walter for ten months, and has never seen him on a call. He writes: '⟦I have been stopped at the border, and the guard says I must pay a $780 fine before they let me through⟧. ⟦Please send it to this account⟧.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:
- What is it asking you to do right now? **Pay or send money**
- What does the request say the money is for? **An investment or an emergency of someone you know only online**

**You are asked, in order:** What does it ask you to do with the money? → Name it.

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Romance scam**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay for an emergency of someone you have never met.** She is asked to pay for his emergency, a fine at a border: “I have been stopped at the border, and the guard says I must pay a $780 fine before they let me through”. Nothing is to be invested.
  - Why not **Pig-butchering scam**: Walter does not show her a site or an app to put money into. The money is for his own trouble.
- If you miss the name or any question, you see the right name, the reason for every question you answered (the first wrong one first), the “why not” line above, and then:
  - If you chose **Put it into a trading site or app that they showed you**: Walter does not show her a site or an app to put money into. The money is for his own trouble.
  - If you chose any other answer, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “You chose «that answer». Give that answer when «what a case must show for it». This case shows something else: «what a case must show for the right answer».”
  - If you chose **Pig-butchering scam**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both begin with someone you have only ever known through messages, often for weeks or months, and both end in a request for a large sum. The same person often runs one after the other. **Romance scam** asks you to pay for trouble that the person says is their own, such as a hospital bill or a ticket home. **Pig-butchering scam** asks you to invest: to put your money into a platform that the person showed you, or to pay a charge to that platform before your money can come out. Is the money to pay for something that has gone wrong for the person, or is it to be invested in a platform that they pointed me to?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”
  - A right name with a wrong answer on the way is shown as “Right name, wrong answer on the way” and counts as a miss.
- Taught on: “An online partner’s emergency that needs your money” (one tap opens the card).

**Drill item 47 of 85**

> Ibrahim paid a $900 deposit for a vacation villa that did not exist. A week later a stranger messages him: 'I run a recovery service. ⟦I can have your $900 returned⟧ ⟦if you send a $90 start-up fee first⟧.'

Shown to you, with the words that decide each answer marked:
- What is it asking you to do right now? **Pay or send money**
- What does the request say the money is for? **Getting back money you lost**

**You are asked, in order:** What does it ask you to do with the money? → Name it.

**Shown as soon as you answer**

- If you are right: “Right: **Recovery scam**.” What does it ask you to do with the money? **Pay a fee before the money reaches you.** There is a fee that comes first: “if you send a $90 start-up fee first”.
  - Why not **Advance-fee scam**: The $900 was not a prize that was never his. It is his own deposit, which he paid and lost.
- If you miss the name or any question, you see the right name, the reason for every question you answered (the first wrong one first), the “why not” line above, and then:

  - If you chose **Advance-fee scam**, the look-alike card’s lines follow: Both are about money that is said to be waiting to be released to you, and both ask for a fee before you can have it. In **Advance-fee scam** the money was never yours: a prize, a grant, a loan or an inheritance. In **Recovery scam** the money was yours and was taken from you earlier, and someone offers to get it back. Is the money that is said to be waiting something that was never mine, or something that I lost earlier?
  - If you chose another name, the line is built from the answers’ own wording: “«The name you chose» needs «what you must be able to point to for it». This case shows something else: «the same line for the right name».”
  - A right name with a wrong answer on the way is shown as “Right name, wrong answer on the way” and counts as a miss.

  - Why not **Fake payment link**: The payment is not on a page that she reaches through a link in a message. It is inside the school’s own app, which she already had.
